Global maximum principle for optimal control of stochastic Volterra equations with singular kernels: An infinite dimensional approach
Yushi Hamaguchi

Abstract
In this paper, we consider optimal control problems of stochastic Volterra equations (SVEs) with singular kernels, where the control domain is not necessarily convex. We establish a global maximum principle by means of the spike variation technique. To do so, we first show a Taylor type expansion of the controlled SVE with respect to the spike variation, where the convergence rates of the remainder terms are characterized by the singularity of the kernels. Next, assuming additional structure conditions for the kernels, we convert the variational SVEs appearing in the expansion to their infinite dimensional lifts. Then, we derive first and second order adjoint equations in form of infinite dimensional backward stochastic evolution equations (BSEEs) on weighted L 2 L^{2} spaces. Moreover, we show the well-posedness of the new class of BSEEs on weighted L 2 L^{2} spaces in a general setting.



Keywords:
global maximum principle; stochastic Volterra equation; infinite dimensional lift; backward stochastic evolution equation.

2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
: 93E20; 49K45; 60H20; 45D05.

1
Introduction

Let
W
be a one-dimensional Brownian motion on a complete probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
, and let
\mathbb{F}=(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be the augmentation of the filtration generated by
W
. Fix
T>0
. Let
\mathcal{U}
be a set of progressively measurable processes
u=(u_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
taking values in a topological space
U
. For each
u\in\mathcal{U}
, consider the following (
n
-dimensional)
controlled stochastic Volterra equation
(SVE):

X^{u}_{t}=\xi_{t}+\int^{t}_{0}K_{b}(t-s)b(s,u_{s},X^{u}_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s+\int^{t}_{0}K_{\sigma}(t-s)\sigma(s,u_{s},X^{u}_{s})\,\mathrm{d}W_{s},\ t\in[0,T].

(1.1)

Here,
b,\sigma:\Omega\times[0,T]\times U\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
are progressively measurable maps,
K_{b},K_{\sigma}:(0,T)\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
are matrix valued deterministic functions called kernels, and
\xi:\Omega\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a given adapted process which represents the forcing term. Each
u\in\mathcal{U}
is referred to as a control process, and the solution
X^{u}
of (
1.1
) is referred to as the state process associated with
u
. Consider a cost functional of the form

J(u):=\mathbb{E}\left[h(X^{u}_{T})+\int^{T}_{0}f(t,u_{t},X^{u}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t\right],

(1.2)

where
h:\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
and
f:\Omega\times[0,T]\times U\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
stand for the terminal and running costs, respectively. We are concerned with the minimization problem for
J(u)
over all control processes
u\in\mathcal{U}
. Specifically, we are interested in the characterization of the (open-loop) optimal control
\hat{u}\in\mathcal{U}
, which is a control process such that
J(\hat{u})=\inf_{u\in\mathcal{U}}J(u)
.

If
K_{b}(t)=K_{\sigma}(t)=I_{n\times n}
and if
\xi_{t}=\xi_{0}
for any
t\in[0,T]
, the controlled SVE (
1.1
) reduces to the following standard controlled stochastic differential equation (SDE):

\mathrm{d}X^{u}_{t}=b(t,u_{t},X^{u}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\sigma(t,u_{t},X^{u}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}W_{t},\ \ t\in[0,T],\ \ X^{u}_{0}=\xi_{0}.

(1.3)

More generally, the SVE (
1.1
) includes a class of (time-) fractional SDEs of the form

\partial^{\beta}_{t}(X^{u}_{t}-\xi_{t})=b(t,u_{t},X^{u}_{t})+\partial^{\gamma}_{t}\int^{t}_{0}\sigma(s,u_{s},X^{u}_{s})\,\mathrm{d}W_{s},\ \ t\in[0,T],\ \ X^{u}_{0}=\xi_{0}.

(1.4)

Here, for
\alpha\in(0,1)
,
\partial^{\alpha}_{t}
is the Caputo fractional derivative of order
\alpha
and is defined by

\partial^{\alpha}_{t}f(t):=\frac{1}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\int^{t}_{0}(t-s)^{-\alpha}(f(s)-f(0))\,\mathrm{d}s

for suitable function
f
such that the integral in the right-hand side is (weakly) differentiable in
t
, where
\Gamma(\lambda):=\int^{\infty}_{0}t^{\lambda-1}e^{-t}\,\mathrm{d}t
,
\lambda>0
, is the Gamma function. It is known that (see
[
7
,
22
,
24
]
), when the parameters
\beta,\gamma\in(0,1)
satisfy
\beta-\gamma>-\frac{1}{2}
, the fractional SDE (
1.4
) is equivalent to

X^{u}_{t}=\xi_{t}+\frac{1}{\Gamma(\beta)}\int^{t}_{0}(t-s)^{\beta-1}b(s,u_{s},X^{u}_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s+\frac{1}{\Gamma(1+\beta-\gamma)}\int^{t}_{0}(t-s)^{\beta-\gamma}\sigma(s,u_{s},X^{u}_{s})\,\mathrm{d}W_{s},\ \ t\in[0,T].

This equation is a special case of the controlled SVE (
1.1
), where the kernels
K_{b}
and
K_{\sigma}
are of the form
K_{b}(t)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\beta)}t^{\beta-1}I_{n\times n}
and
K_{\sigma}(t)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(1+\beta-\gamma)}t^{\beta-\gamma}I_{n\times n}
, respectively. The fractional SDE captures the memory effect and heredity, and is useful to describe anomalous diffusions exhibiting subdiffusive behavior
[
7
,
22
,
24
]
. Fractional calculus has attracted lots of attentions in several fields of applied mathematics such as physics, chemistry, engineering, and mathematical finance. Fractional optimal control problems have been extensively studied in the deterministic setting; see for example
[
4
,
5
,
10
,
19
,
20
,
21
]
and references cited therein. It is important to consider the stochastic version of the fractional optimal control when the effect of randomness to the fractional controlled system is crucial. This motivates us to investigate the
stochastic fractional control
or more generally the
stochastic Volterra control with singular kernels
; we say that the kernels
K_{b}
and
K_{\sigma}
in the controlled SVE (
1.1
) are singular if they are unbounded on a neighbourhood of zero. Notice that the weakest conditions for the singular kernels which make the Lebesgue and stochastic integrals in (
1.1
) well-defined are that
K_{b}\in L^{1}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
and
K_{\sigma}\in L^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
. Typical examples of such singular kernels are the
fractional kernels
of the form
K_{b}(t)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\beta_{b})}t^{\beta_{b}-1}I_{n\times n}
and
K_{\sigma}(t)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\beta_{\sigma})}t^{\beta_{\sigma}-1}I_{n\times n}
with
\beta_{b}\in(0,1)
and
\beta_{\sigma}\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
, which appear in the integral form of the fractional SDE (
1.4
).

In this paper, we establish a
global maximum principle
for the stochastic Volterra control problem (
1.1
)–(
1.2
) with singular kernels and general (not necessarily convex) control domain
U
. The maximum principle is one of the most important principles in (stochastic) control theory. The global/local maximum principle provides a necessary condition for optimality in terms of the global/local maximization of the associated (risk adjusted) Hamiltonian. Now let us briefly review the well-established theory of the maximum principle for the control problem of SDE (
1.3
)–(
1.2
), which corresponds to (
1.1
) with
K_{b}(t)=K_{\sigma}(t)=I_{n\times n}
and
\xi_{t}=\xi_{0}
. In this SDE setting, Peng
[
27
]
established a “global” maximum principle, which states that, under suitable assumptions, every optimal control
\hat{u}\in\mathcal{U}
of the problem (
1.3
)–(
1.2
) must satisfy the following variational inequality:

\begin{split}&H(t,\hat{u}_{t},\hat{X}_{t},\hat{p}_{t},\hat{q}_{t})-H(t,v,\hat{X}_{t},\hat{p}_{t},\hat{q}_{t})-\frac{1}{2}\big\langle\hat{P}_{t}\big\{\sigma(t,\hat{u}_{t},\hat{X}_{t})-\sigma(t,v,\hat{X}_{t})\big\},\sigma(t,\hat{u}_{t},\hat{X}_{t})-\sigma(t,v,\hat{X}_{t})\big\rangle\geq 0\\
&\hskip 170.71652pt\text{for all $v\in U$ a.s.\ for a.e.\ $t\in[0,T]$.}\end{split}

(1.5)

Here,
H:\Omega\times[0,T]\times U\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
is the
Hamiltonian
defined by

H(t,u,x,p,q):=\langle p,b(t,u,x)\rangle+\langle q,\sigma(t,u,x)\rangle-f(t,u,x),

(1.6)

\hat{X}=X^{\hat{u}}
is the corresponding (optimal) state process,
\hat{p}
and
\hat{q}
are the corresponding
first order adjoint processes
, and
\hat{P}
, together with an auxiliary process
\hat{Q}
, are the corresponding
second order adjoint processes
. The pair
(\hat{p},\hat{q})
is defined as the adapted solution of the following backward stochastic differential equation (BSDE) on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
:

\begin{cases}\mathrm{d}\hat{p}_{t}=-H_{x}(t,\hat{u}_{t},\hat{X}_{t},\hat{p}_{t},\hat{q}_{t})^{\top}\,\mathrm{d}t+\hat{q}_{t}\,\mathrm{d}W_{t},\ \ t\in[0,T],\\
\hat{p}_{T}=-h_{x}(\hat{X}_{T})^{\top}.\end{cases}

(1.7)

The pair
(\hat{P},\hat{Q})
is defined as the adapted solution of the following BSDE on
\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
:

\begin{cases}\mathrm{d}\hat{P}_{t}=-\Big\{b_{x}(t,\hat{u}_{t},\hat{X}_{t})^{\top}\hat{P}_{t}+\hat{P}_{t}b_{x}(t,\hat{u}_{t},\hat{X}_{t})+\sigma_{x}(t,\hat{u}_{t},\hat{X}_{t})^{\top}\hat{P}_{t}\sigma_{x}(t,\hat{u}_{t},\hat{X}_{t})\\
\hskip 85.35826pt+\sigma_{x}(t,\hat{u}_{t},\hat{X}_{t})^{\top}\hat{Q}_{t}+\hat{Q}_{t}\sigma_{x}(t,\hat{u}_{t},\hat{X}_{t})+H_{xx}(t,\hat{u}_{t},\hat{X}_{t},\hat{p}_{t},\hat{q}_{t})\Big\}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
\hskip 28.45274pt+\hat{Q}_{t}\,\mathrm{d}W_{t},\ \ t\in[0,T],\\
\hat{P}_{T}=-h_{xx}(\hat{X}_{T}).\end{cases}

(1.8)

Here and elsewhere,
\varphi_{x}
and
\varphi_{xx}
denote the derivative and Hessian matrix of a function
\varphi
with respect to the state variable
x
. For the theory and applications of BSDEs, see the textbook
[
40
]
. The BSDEs (
1.7
) and (
1.8
) are called the
first order adjoint equation
and
second order adjoint equation
, respectively, associated with the optimal control
\hat{u}
. The first order adjoint processes
(\hat{p},\hat{q})
can be seen as an analogue of the generalized momenta in the classical mechanics; see
[
39
, Chapter 5]
. In the variational inequality (
1.5
), the process
\hat{P}
plays a role of the risk adjustment for the Hamiltonian maximization, which is related to the second order effect of the control on the diffusion coefficient
\sigma(t,u,x)
and reflects the controller’s risk-averse or risk-seeking attitude; see
[
39
, Chapter 3]
. In some special cases, the global maximum principle (
1.5
) implies the following consequences.

•

If the diffusion coefficient
\sigma
does not depend on the control variable
u
, then the global maximum principle (
1.5
) reduces to the following necessary optimality condition:

H(t,\hat{u}_{t},\hat{X}_{t},\hat{p}_{t},\hat{q}_{t})=\max_{v\in U}H(t,v,\hat{X}_{t},\hat{p}_{t},\hat{q}_{t})\ \ \text{a.s.\ for a.e.\ $t\in[0,T]$.}

•

If the control domain
U
is a convex body of a Euclidean space and if
b,\sigma,f
are continuously differentiable with respect to the control variable
u
, then (
1.5
) implies the following weaker version of necessary optimality condition:

H_{u}(t,\hat{u}_{t},\hat{X}_{t},\hat{p}_{t},\hat{q}_{t})(v-\hat{u}_{t})\leq 0\ \ \text{for all $v\in U$ a.s.\ for a.e.\ $t\in[0,T]$,}

where
H_{u}
denotes the derivative of the Hamiltonian
H
with respect to the control variable
u
. The above is referred to as the “local” maximum principle.

In the above two special cases, the contribution of the risk adjustment term
\hat{P}
disappears, and we do not need to introduce the second order adjoint equation (
1.8
). Under the convexity assumption on
U
as in the second bullet point above, the local maximum principle (which is a weaker necessary optimality condition than the global maximum principle) can be directly obtained via the analysis of the first order perturbation of the system in terms of the
convex variation
of the control, which is a variation of the given (optimal) control
\hat{u}
of the form
\hat{u}+\varepsilon(v-\hat{u})
with small parameter
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and arbitrary control process
v\in\mathcal{U}
. However, this technique can not derive the global maximum principle and is not applicable when the control domain
U
is not convex (such as
U=\{0,1\}
). In order to derive the global maximum principle allowing the control domain to be non-convex, one should consider the perturbation of the system in terms of the
spike variation
of the control, which is of the form
\hat{u}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[0,T]\setminus[\tau,\tau+\varepsilon]}+v\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\tau,\tau+\varepsilon]}
with arbitrary
\tau\in[0,T)
,
v\in\mathcal{U}
and small parameter
\varepsilon\in(0,T-\tau]
. For more detailed theory, interpretations and history of the maximum principle for SDEs, see
[
39
, Chapters 3 and 5]
.

In the literature of deterministic fractional/Volterra optimal control, global maximum principles were established by several authors, for example in
[
19
,
20
,
21
]
. A local maximum principle for (non-convolution type) SVEs was first investigated by Yong
[
37
]
and rigorously proved by his subsequent paper
[
38
]
. He assumed that the kernels are regular (that is, bounded) and that the control domain
U
is a convex subset of a Euclidean space. In this setting, utilizing the convex variation technique, he obtained the corresponding first order adjoint equation in form of a (Type-II) backward stochastic Volterra integral equation (BSVIE), and established a local maximum principle. After that, generalizations and variants of
[
37
,
38
]
have been established in
[
3
,
31
,
30
,
32
]
for regular kernel cases and in
[
11
,
12
,
36
]
for singular kernel cases, among others. These works rely on the convexity assumption on the control domain
U
and consider only the local maximum principle by means of the convex variation technique. The analysis of the global maximum principle for the stochastic Volterra control problem is quite limited. To the best of the author’s knowledge, there are only two essential works
[
34
,
35
]
in this direction so far (see also
[
26
]
for the application of the technique established in
[
34
,
35
]
to control problems of SDEs with delay). In
[
34
,
35
]
, the authors considered stochastic Volterra control problems with (non-convolution type) regular kernels and general (not necessarily convex) control domain. Wang
[
34
]
derived a global maximum principle by means of a first order adjoint process solving a Type-II BSVIE and two implicit operator-valued second order adjoint processes. After that, the second order adjoint equation in form of a system of non-standard BSVIEs was derived by Wang and Yong
[
35
]
. However, their works heavily rely on the regularity of the kernels, and their generalization to the singular kernel case is quite non-trivial. In particular, they are not applicable to the stochastic fractional control problem described by (
1.4
)–(
1.2
).

In this paper, we generalize Peng’s global maximum principle (
1.5
) to the stochastic Volterra control problem (
1.1
)–(
1.2
) with singular kernels and general control domain. We utilize the spike variation technique, together with the technique of
infinite dimensional lifts
of SVEs in the spirit of the author’s previous works
[
13
,
14
]
. The method in the present paper is completely different from the aforementioned works on maximum principle for fractional optimal control and stochastic Volterra control problems. We provide a new infinite dimensional perspective on the global maximum principle for the stochastic Volterra control problem, allowing the kernels to be singular. Noteworthy is that we do not rely on the analysis of fractional derivatives or BSVIEs.

The main contributions of this paper are the following three points:

(i)

We first show novel estimates for the Taylor type expansion of the controlled SVE (
1.1
) with respect to the spike variation, where the convergence rates of the remainder terms are characterized by the singularity of the kernels. See
Proposition

3.1
.

(ii)

Under additional structure conditions on the kernels, we derive new types of first and second order adjoint equations in form of
infinite dimensional backward stochastic evolution equations (BSEEs) on weighted
L^{2}
spaces
, and obtain a global maximum principle. See
Section

4
.

(iii)

We prove the well-posedness of a general class of BSEEs on weighted
L^{2}
spaces, which is also new and includes the first and second order adjoint equations as special cases. See
Section

4.3
and
Section

5
.

The main result is
Theorem

4.15
, that is summarized in the contribution (ii) above. The contributions (i) and (iii) are secondary to the purpose of establishing the global maximum principle for stochastic Volterra control problems with singular kernels, but they provide important and novel findings by their own rights beyond stochastic Volterra control theory. Here, let us make further explanations on the above contributions.

On the contribution (i).

In order to derive the global/local maximum principles, the important first step is to investigate the effect of the variation of the control to the state process. If one considers the convex variation
\hat{u}+\varepsilon(v-\hat{u})
(assuming that the control domain
U
is convex), the perturbation
X^{\hat{u}+\varepsilon(v-\hat{u})}
from the optimal state process
\hat{X}:=X^{\hat{u}}
can be estimated by the standard variational argument; see
[
38
]
. This is related to the regularity in the control variable, but the regularity in time is not crucial. Hence, the singularity of the kernels does not matter for deriving the local maximum principle via the convex variation, and this is the reason why parallel arguments of
[
38
]
(for the regular kernel cases) are successfully applicable even in the singular kernel cases in
[
11
,
12
,
36
]
. However, this is not the case when one wants to derive the global maximum principle via the spike variation
\hat{u}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[0,T]\setminus[\tau,\tau+\varepsilon]}+v\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\tau,\tau+\varepsilon]}
under general control domain
U
. Unlike the convex variation case, the regularity in time of the controlled system is crucial in the estimate of the perturbation
X^{\hat{u}{\scriptsize\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.04861pt\mbox{\rm{l}}}_{[0,T]\setminus[\tau,\tau+\varepsilon]}+v{\scriptsize\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.04861pt\mbox{\rm{l}}}_{[\tau,\tau+\varepsilon]}}
from
\hat{X}
. Intuitively speaking, in the deterministic ordinary differential equation case, the estimate of the spike variation is related to the time-regularity of the Lebesgue integral of the form
\int^{t}_{0}\{\cdots\}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\tau,\tau+\varepsilon]}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s
. This is of order
O(\varepsilon)
, and hence the first order variation is sufficient to obtain the order
o(\varepsilon)
for the remainder term. In the SDE case (with controlled diffusion), in addition to the Lebesgue integral term, we need to estimate the time-regularity of the stochastic integral of the form
\int^{t}_{0}\{\cdots\}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\tau,\tau+\varepsilon]}(s)\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}
. The latter is of order
O(\varepsilon^{1/2})
in
L^{p}
, and this is the reason why the second order variation is necessary (and sufficient) in this SDE case to obtain the order
o(\varepsilon)
for the remainder term; see
[
27
]
and
[
39
, Chapter 3]
. Analogously, in the case of stochastic Volterra control problem, the time-regularity of the Lebesgue Volterra integral
\int^{t}_{0}K_{b}(t-s)\{\cdots\}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\tau,\tau+\varepsilon]}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s
and the stochastic Volterra integral
\int^{t}_{0}K_{\sigma}(t-s)\{\cdots\}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\tau,\tau+\varepsilon]}(s)\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}
are crucial. On the one hand, if the kernels
K_{b}
and
K_{\sigma}
are sufficiently regular, it turns out that these Volterra type integrals have the same orders as that without kernels; see
[
34
,
35
]
. On the other hand, if the kernels are singular, the Volterra integrals have lower time-regularity than the standard Lebesgue and stochastic integrals. Consequently, variational estimates with respect to the spike variation are quite sensitive in the singular kernel cases. With these observations in mind, we provide variational estimates in terms of the singularity of the kernels
K_{b}
and
K_{\sigma}
. In particular, it turns out that the remainder term by the second order expansion becomes of order
o(\varepsilon)
if
K_{b}\in L^{3/2}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
and
K_{\sigma}\in L^{6}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
; see
Proposition

3.1
. This is the first important contribution of the present paper and is crucial for deriving the global maximum principle.

On the contribution (ii).

By the Taylor type expansion in (i), one gets a first order expansion of the cost functional in form of
J(\hat{u}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[0,T]\setminus[\tau,\tau+\varepsilon]}+v\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\tau,\tau+\varepsilon]})=J(\hat{u})+J^{1,2,v,\tau,\varepsilon}+o(\varepsilon)
, where
J^{1,2,v,\tau,\varepsilon}
is defined as a linear-quadratic functional of the solutions
X^{1,v,\tau,\varepsilon}
and
X^{2,v,\tau,\varepsilon}
to the first and second order variational (linear) SVEs appearing in the expansion. From this, we immediately obtain a necessary condition
\liminf_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}J^{1,2,v,\tau,\varepsilon}\geq 0
for
\hat{u}
to be optimal. However, this is still implicit and is not easily applicable, since the term
J^{1,2,v,\tau,\varepsilon}
involves
X^{1,v,\tau,\varepsilon}
and
X^{2,v,\tau,\varepsilon}
, which implicitly depend on
v
. The next important step to derive the global maximum principle is to introduce precise forms of adjoint equations that enable us to get rid of
X^{1,v,\tau,\varepsilon}
and
X^{2,v,\tau,\varepsilon}
from the expression of
J^{1,2,v,\tau,\varepsilon}
via a kind of duality formula. In the SDE setting,
X^{1,v,\tau,\varepsilon}
and
X^{2,v,\tau,\varepsilon}
are Itô processes solving variational SDEs, and hence we can use powerful tools of Itô’s calculus and BSDE theory as demonstrated in
[
27
]
. However, this is not the case in the SVE setting. The biggest difficulty in the Volterra case is that the variational processes
X^{1,v,\tau,\varepsilon}
and
X^{2,v,\tau,\varepsilon}
, solutions to the corresponding variational SVEs, are no longer semimartingales. Hence, we can not use Itô’s calculus or BSDE theory directly. In order to overcome this difficulty, our idea is to utilize the framework of infinite dimensional lifts of SVEs investigated in
[
13
,
14
]
. This makes us possible to express
X^{1,v,\tau,\varepsilon}
and
X^{2,v,\tau,\varepsilon}
as super positions of infinitely many semimartingales. By using this idea, we can successfully calculate the term
J^{1,2,v,\tau,\varepsilon}
and obtain first and second order adjoint equations in the infinite dimensional framework.

Let us briefly summarize the main result (
Theorem

4.15
) related to the contribution (ii). We assume that the kernels
K_{b}
and
K_{\sigma}
are of the following forms:

K_{b}(t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}e^{-\theta t}M_{b}(\theta)\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\ \ \text{and}\ \ K_{\sigma}(t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}e^{-\theta t}M_{\sigma}(\theta)\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta),\ \ t\in(0,T),

(1.9)

for some Borel measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}=[0,\infty)
and matrix-valued measurable maps
M_{b},M_{\sigma}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
satisfying suitable integrability conditions (see
Assumption

2
for more details). The fractional kernel is an important and typical example satisfying the above structure condition. Under this setting (with suitable conditions), we establish the following global maximum principle: every optimal control
\hat{u}\in\mathcal{U}
of the stochastic Volterra control problem (
1.1
)–(
1.2
) must satisfy the following variational inequality:

\displaystyle H\big(t,\hat{u}_{t},\hat{X}_{t},\mu[M_{b}^{\top}\hat{p}_{t}],\mu[M_{\sigma}^{\top}\hat{q}_{t}]\big)-H\big(t,v,\hat{X}_{t},\mu[M_{b}^{\top}\hat{p}_{t}],\mu[M_{\sigma}^{\top}\hat{q}_{t}]\big)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\Big\langle\mu^{\otimes 2}[M_{\sigma}^{\top}\hat{P}_{t}M_{\sigma}]\big\{\sigma(t,\hat{u}_{t},\hat{X}_{t})-\sigma(t,v,\hat{X}_{t})\big\},\sigma(t,\hat{u}_{t},\hat{X}_{t})-\sigma(t,v,\hat{X}_{t})\Big\rangle\geq 0

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\text{for all $v\in U$ a.s.\ for a.e.\ $t\in[0,T]$}.

Here,
H
is the Hamiltonian defined by (
1.6
), which is exactly the same as the one in the classical SDE case.
\hat{X}=X^{\hat{u}}
is the corresponding optimal state process, that is the solution of the controlled SVE (
1.1
) associated with the optimal control
\hat{u}
. The terms
\mu[M_{b}^{\top}\hat{p}_{t}]
and
\mu[M_{\sigma}^{\top}\hat{q}_{t}]
are regarded as the first order adjoint processes, which are defined by

\mu[M_{b}^{\top}\hat{p}_{t}]:=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}M_{b}(\theta)^{\top}\hat{p}_{t}(\theta)\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\ \ \text{and}\ \ \mu[M_{\sigma}^{\top}\hat{q}_{t}]:=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}M_{\sigma}(\theta)^{\top}\hat{q}_{t}(\theta)\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta),

and the pair of random fields
\hat{p},\hat{q}:\Omega\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is defined as the solution of the following first order adjoint equation:

\begin{cases}\mathrm{d}\hat{p}_{t}(\theta)=\theta\hat{p}_{t}(\theta)\,\mathrm{d}t-H_{x}\big(t,\hat{u}_{t},\hat{X}_{t},\mu[M_{b}^{\top}\hat{p}_{t}],\mu[M_{\sigma}^{\top}\hat{q}_{t}]\big)^{\top}\,\mathrm{d}t+\hat{q}_{t}(\theta)\,\mathrm{d}W_{t},\ \ \theta\in\mathbb{R}_{+},\ t\in[0,T],\\
\hat{p}_{T}(\theta)=-h_{x}(\hat{X}_{T})^{\top},\ \ \theta\in\mathbb{R}_{+}.\end{cases}

(1.10)

The term
\mu^{\otimes 2}[M_{\sigma}^{\top}\hat{P}_{t}M_{\sigma}]
is regarded as the second order adjoint process or the risk adjustment term, which is defined by

\mu^{\otimes 2}[M_{\sigma}^{\top}\hat{P}_{t}M_{\sigma}]:=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}M_{\sigma}(\theta_{1})^{\top}\hat{P}_{t}(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})M_{\sigma}(\theta_{2})\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta_{1})\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta_{2}),

and the pair of a random field
\hat{P}:\Omega\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
and an auxiliary random field
\hat{Q}:\Omega\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is defined as the solution of the following second order adjoint equation:

\begin{cases}\mathrm{d}\hat{P}_{t}(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})=(\theta_{1}+\theta_{2})\hat{P}_{t}(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})\,\mathrm{d}t\\
\hskip 56.9055pt-\Big\{b_{x}(t,\hat{u}_{t},\hat{X}_{t})^{\top}\mu[M_{b}^{\top}\hat{P}_{t}(\cdot,\theta_{2})]+\mu[\hat{P}_{t}(\theta_{1},\cdot)M_{b}]b_{x}(t,\hat{u}_{t},\hat{X}_{t})\\
\hskip 99.58464pt+\sigma_{x}(t,\hat{u}_{t},\hat{X}_{t})^{\top}\mu^{\otimes 2}[M_{\sigma}^{\top}\hat{P}_{t}M_{\sigma}]\sigma_{x}(t,\hat{u}_{t},\hat{X}_{t})\\
\hskip 99.58464pt+\sigma_{x}(t,\hat{u}_{t},\hat{X}_{t})^{\top}\mu[M_{\sigma}^{\top}\hat{Q}_{t}(\cdot,\theta_{2})]+\mu[\hat{Q}_{t}(\theta_{1},\cdot)M_{\sigma}]\sigma_{x}(t,\hat{u}_{t},\hat{X}_{t})\\
\hskip 99.58464pt+H_{xx}\big(t,\hat{u}_{t},\hat{X}_{t},\mu[M_{b}^{\top}\hat{p}_{t}],\mu[M_{\sigma}^{\top}\hat{q}_{t}]\big)\Big\}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
\hskip 56.9055pt+\hat{Q}_{t}(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})\,\mathrm{d}W_{t},\ \ (\theta_{1},\theta_{2})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2},\ t\in[0,T],\\
\hat{P}_{T}(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})=-h_{xx}(\hat{X}_{T}),\ \ (\theta_{1},\theta_{2})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}.\end{cases}

(1.11)

The first and second order adjoint equations (
1.10
) and (
1.11
) above are systems of infinitely many finite dimensional BSDEs, which are parametrized by
\theta
and
(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})
, respectively, and coupled via the integration with respect to the measure
\mu
. In another perspective, these are formulated as
infinite dimensional BSEEs on weighted
L^{2}
spaces
. The random fields
\hat{p}
and
\hat{q}
can be regarded as the
lifted first order adjoint processes
; the first order adjoint processes
\mu[M_{b}^{\top}\hat{p}_{t}]
and
\mu[M_{\sigma}^{\top}\hat{q}_{t}]
are realised as their super positions reflecting the first order effects of the kernels
K_{b}
and
K_{\sigma}
. Similarly, the random field
\hat{P}
can be regarded as the
lifted risk adjustment term
; the risk adjustment term
\mu^{\otimes 2}[M_{\sigma}^{\top}\hat{P}_{t}M_{\sigma}]
is realized as its superposition reflecting the second order effect of the kernel
K_{\sigma}
on the diffusion. Notice the simple analogy of our global maximum principle to the classical one in the SDE setting. In the SDE setting, which corresponds to (
1.1
) with
K_{b}(t)=K_{\sigma}(t)=I_{n\times n}
and
\xi_{t}=\xi_{0}
, we can take
\mu=\delta_{0}
and
M_{b}(\theta)=M_{\sigma}(\theta)=I_{n\times n}
. Then, the first and second order adjoint equations (
1.10
) and (
1.11
) with
\theta=0
and
(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})=(0,0)
reduce to the counterparts (
1.7
) and (
1.8
), respectively, and we immediately recover the classical global maximum principle (
1.5
) in the SDE setting. The adjoint equations (
1.10
) and (
1.11
) are much simpler than that of
[
35
]
obtained in the (non-convolution type) regular kernel cases. The second order adjoint equation derived in
[
35
]
is a quite complicated coupled system of BSVIEs, which consists of 4 equations involving non-standard Volterra structures. These BSVIE-type adjoint equations are actually not straightforward generalizations of the counterparts (
1.7
) and (
1.8
) in the SDE setting, and some technical transforms are necessary to recover the classical result; see
[
35
, Section 6.2]
. Compared to
[
35
]
, our adjoint equations are tractable thanks to the semimartingale structure behind them and are natural generalizations of the counterparts in the SDE setting. As a price, in our study, the structure condition (
1.9
) is crucial. In
Appendix

B
, we investigate relationships between our BSEE-type adjoint equations and the BSVIE-type adjoint equations obtained in
[
35
]
.

The idea of lifting SVEs to infinite dimensional Markovian systems originates from the work of Carmona and Coutin
[
6
]
, where the authors study the Markovian lift of Riemann–Liouville fractional Brownian motion in terms of an infinite dimensional Ornstein–Uhlenbeck type process. Since then, general theory on infinite dimensional lifts of SVEs in the spirit of
[
6
]
has been studied in
[
1
,
8
,
9
,
13
,
14
,
15
]
in different frameworks. Especially among them, the approach established in the author’s previous works
[
13
,
14
]
makes us possible to study the infinite dimensional lifts of SVEs in weighted
L^{2}
spaces, which are tractable thanks to the Hilbert space structure. In the present paper, we adopt (and slightly generalize) the idea of
[
13
,
14
]
. It is worth to remark that Abi Jaber, Miller and Pham
[
1
]
applied the technique of infinite dimensional lifts in the
L^{1}
space to the study of linear-quadratic stochastic Volterra control problem without terminal cost. They characterized the optimal control by means of an infinite dimensional operator-valued Riccati equation (whose well-posedness was shown in their subsequent work
[
2
]
), which has a similar structure to the second order adjoint equation (
1.11
) derived in the present paper.

On the contribution (iii).

As mentioned above, the adjoint equations (
1.10
) and (
1.11
) can be formulated as infinite dimensional BSEEs on weighted
L^{2}
spaces. Actually, if the kernels are regular, it turns out that the corresponding adjoint equations reduce to “standard” BSEEs on Gelfand triplets of Hilbert spaces, and the well-established result in
[
28
]
is applicable; see also
[
17
,
18
,
23
,
25
,
42
]
for related works on infinite dimensional BSEEs on Gelfand triplets. However, a difficulty appears again in the singular kernel case, where the adjoint equations (
1.10
) and (
1.11
) are beyond the existing framework of BSEEs mentioned above; see
Remark

4.9
. In
Section

4.3
and
Section

5
, we formulate a new class of BSEEs on weighted
L^{2}
spaces, which is beyond the Gelfand triplet framework and includes the adjoint equations (
1.10
) and (
1.11
) in the singular kernel cases. We show in
Theorem

4.8
its well-posedness. This result provides an important new finding in theory of BSDEs and BSEEs.

The rest of this paper is organized as follows. In
Section

2
, we summarize notations we use throughout this paper, provide preliminary results, and formulate the stochastic Volterra control problem (
1.1
)–(
1.2
) giving precise assumptions.
Section

3
corresponds to the contribution (i) mentioned above, where we show the Taylor type expansion of the controlled SVE (
1.1
) in terms of the spike variation.
Section

4
corresponds to the contribution (ii), which is the main part of this paper. We introduce the framework of infinite dimensional lifts of variational SVEs following
[
13
]
in
Section

4.1
, heuristically explain how to derive the adjoint equations (
1.10
) and (
1.11
) in
Section

4.2
, formulate the new class of BSEEs on weighted
L^{2}
-spaces including the adjoint equations in
Section

4.3
, and rigorously prove the global maximum principle in
Section

4.4
.
Section

5
corresponds to the contribution (iii), where we prove well-posedness of general BSEEs on weighted
L^{2}
spaces. In
Appendix

A
, we make some remarks on measurability issues arising in the study of BSEEs. In
Appendix

B
, we show some relationships between our BSEE-type adjoint equations and the BSVIE-type adjoint equations obtained in
[
35
]
.

2
Notations, preliminaries and problem formulation

2.1
Notations

Throughout this paper, we fix a complete probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
and a one-dimensional Brownian motion
W
on it; the one-dimensionality of Brownian motion
W
is just for simplicity of notations, and all results in this paper can be easily generalized to the multi-dimensional case. We denote by
\mathbb{F}=(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
the augmentation of the filtration generated by
W
. We also fix
T>0
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, that stand for the terminal time and the dimension of the state process, respectively.

The Euclidean norm and the standard inner product on a Euclidean space are denoted by
|\cdot|
and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
, respectively. The expectation operator with respect to
\mathbb{P}
is denoted by
\mathbb{E}[\cdot]
, and the conditional expectation operator associated with
\mathcal{F}_{t}
is denoted by
\mathbb{E}_{t}[\cdot]
for each
t\geq 0
. For each set
A
,
\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{A}
denotes the indicator function. Given a measurable space
(A,\mathcal{A})
, we say that a map
\varphi
from
\Omega\times[0,T]\times A
to another measurable space is progressively measurable if for any
t\in[0,T]
, the restriction of
\varphi
to
\Omega\times[0,t]\times A
is
\mathcal{F}_{t}\otimes\mathcal{B}([0,t])\otimes\mathcal{A}
-measurable.

For each sufficiently smooth function
\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
,
\varphi_{x}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{1\times n}
denotes the derivative, and
\varphi_{xx}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
denotes the Hessian matrix. For each
\varphi=(\varphi^{1},\dots,\varphi^{m})^{\top}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
with sufficiently smooth
\varphi^{i}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
, we denote

\varphi_{x}:=\begin{pmatrix}\varphi^{1}_{x}\\
\vdots\\
\varphi^{m}_{x}\end{pmatrix}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}\ \ \text{and}\ \ \varphi_{xx}:=\begin{pmatrix}\varphi^{1}_{xx}\\
\vdots\\
\varphi^{m}_{xx}\end{pmatrix}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{m\times(n\times n)}=\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{R}^{n\times n};\mathbb{R}^{m}).

(2.1)

For each
M=\begin{pmatrix}M^{1}\\
\vdots\\
M^{\ell}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell\times(m\times n)}=\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{R}^{m\times n};\mathbb{R}^{\ell})
with
M^{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
,
k=1,\dots,\ell
, and each vector
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
y\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
z\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
, we write

\langle Mx,y\rangle:=\begin{pmatrix}\langle M^{1}x,y\rangle\\
\vdots\\
\langle M^{\ell}x,y\rangle\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}\ \ \text{and}\ \ \langle z,M\rangle:=\sum^{\ell}_{k=1}z^{k}M^{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}.

(2.2)

Notice that
\langle z,\langle Mx,y\rangle\rangle=\langle\langle z,M\rangle x,y\rangle
.

Let
E
be a Euclidean space. For each
p\in[1,\infty]
and two real numbers
s<t
,
(L^{p}(s,t;E),\|\cdot\|_{L^{p}(s,t)})
denotes the standard Lebesgue space of
L^{p}
-maps from
(0,T)
to
E
. Also, for each
t\geq 0
,
(L^{p}_{\mathcal{F}_{t}}(\Omega;E),\|\cdot\|_{L^{p}_{\mathcal{F}_{t}}(\Omega)})
denotes the
L^{p}
space (with respect to
\mathbb{P}
) of equivalence classes of
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-measurable maps from
\Omega
to
E
. We consider the following classes of stochastic processes:

•

L^{p,\infty}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T;E)
: the set of (equivalence classes of) progressively measurable maps
\xi:\Omega\times[0,T]\to E
such that

\|\xi\|_{L^{p,\infty}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T)}:=\esssup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}\big[|\xi_{t}|^{p}\big]^{1/p}<\infty.

•

C^{p}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T;E)
: the set of (equivalence classes of) progressively measurable maps
\xi:\Omega\times[0,T]\to E
such that
\xi_{t}\in L^{p}_{\mathcal{F}_{t}}(\Omega;E)
for any
t\in[0,T]
and that the map
t\mapsto\xi_{t}
is continuous with respect to the norm in
L^{p}_{\mathcal{F}_{T}}(\Omega;E)
. Denote

\|\xi\|_{C^{p}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T)}:=\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}\big[|\xi_{t}|^{p}\big]^{1/p}.

Lastly, we introduce the following unusual notation. Let
q\in[1,\infty]
and
\varepsilon\in(0,T]
. For each
K\in L^{q}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
, we set

\|K\|_{q,\varepsilon}:=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq s<t\leq T\\
t-s\leq\varepsilon\end{subarray}}\|K\|_{L^{q}(s,t)}.

Notice that
\|\cdot\|_{q,\varepsilon}
is a norm on
L^{q}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
and equivalent to the original norm
\|\cdot\|_{L^{q}(0,T)}
. Also, notice that
\|\cdot\|_{q,T}=\|\cdot\|_{L^{q}(0,T)}
and
\|\cdot\|_{\infty,\varepsilon}=\|\cdot\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T)}
. If
K
is in form of
K(t)=k(t)I_{n\times n}
for some nonnegative and non-increasing scalar function
k:(0,T)\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
(such as the fractional kernel), then
\|K\|_{q,\varepsilon}=\|k\|_{L^{q}(0,\varepsilon)}
. The convergence rate of
\|K\|_{q,\varepsilon}\to 0
as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
corresponds to the regularity and singularity of
K
. The norm
\|\cdot\|_{q,\varepsilon}
plays a crucial role in the variational argument for the controlled SVE (
1.1
) with singular kernels; see
Proposition

3.1
below.

Remark 2.1
.

The following properties are trivial but important:

•

If
K\in L^{q}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
with
q\in[1,\infty)
, then
\|K\|_{q,\varepsilon}\to 0
as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
. This follows from the uniform continuity of the function
t\mapsto\int^{t}_{0}|K(r)|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}r
on
[0,T]
.

•

If
K\in L^{q^{\prime}}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
with
1\leq q\leq q^{\prime}\leq\infty
, then
\|K\|_{q,\varepsilon}\leq\|K\|_{q^{\prime},\varepsilon}\varepsilon^{1/q-1/{q^{\prime}}}
, where
1/\infty:=0
. This follows from Hölder’s inequality.

2.2
Preliminaries

The following lemma is related to the time-regularity of Volterra integrals, which plays a crucial role in
Proposition

3.1
.

Lemma 2.2
.

(i)

Let
K\in L^{1}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
and
p\in[1,\infty)
. Then, the map
b\mapsto(\int^{t}_{0}K(t-s)b_{s}\,\mathrm{d}s)_{t\in[0,T]}
defines a bounded linear operator from
L^{p,\infty}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n})
to
C^{p}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Furthermore, for any
b\in L^{p,\infty}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
\tau\in[0,T)
and
\varepsilon\in(0,T-\tau]
, it holds that

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\int^{t}_{0}K(t-s)b_{s}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\tau,\tau+\varepsilon]}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\right|^{p}\right]^{1/p}\leq\|K\|_{1,\varepsilon}\|b\|_{L^{p,\infty}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T)}.

(2.3)

(ii)

Let
K\in L^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
and
p\in[2,\infty)
. Then, the map
\sigma\mapsto(\int^{t}_{0}K(t-s)\sigma_{s}\,\mathrm{d}W_{s})_{t\in[0,T]}
defines a bounded linear operator from
L^{p,\infty}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n})
to
C^{p}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Furthermore, for any
\sigma\in L^{p,\infty}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
\tau\in[0,T)
and
\varepsilon\in(0,T-\tau]
, it holds that

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\int^{t}_{0}K(t-s)\sigma_{s}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\tau,\tau+\varepsilon]}(s)\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}\right|^{p}\right]^{1/p}\leq C_{p}\|K\|_{2,\varepsilon}\|\sigma\|_{L^{p,\infty}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T)},

(2.4)

where
C_{p}
is a constant depending only on
p
.

Proof.

First, we show the assertion (i). Suppose that
K\in L^{1}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
and
p\in[1,\infty)
. Let
b\in L^{p,\infty}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n})
be fixed. Notice that, for each
0\leq t\leq t^{\prime}\leq T
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\int^{t^{\prime}}_{0}K(t^{\prime}-s)b_{s}\,\mathrm{d}s-\int^{t}_{0}K(t-s)b_{s}\,\mathrm{d}s\right|^{p}\right]^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int^{t^{\prime}}_{t}|K(t^{\prime}-s)||b_{s}|\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{p}\right]^{1/p}+\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int^{t}_{0}|K(t^{\prime}-s)-K(t-s)||b_{s}|\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{p}\right]^{1/p}.

Using Minkowski’s integral inequality, one has

\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int^{t^{\prime}}_{t}|K(t^{\prime}-s)||b_{s}|\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{p}\right]^{1/p}\leq\int^{t^{\prime}}_{t}|K(t^{\prime}-s)|\mathbb{E}\big[|b_{s}|^{p}\big]^{1/p}\,\mathrm{d}s\leq\int^{t^{\prime}}_{t}|K(t^{\prime}-s)|\,\mathrm{d}s\,\|b\|_{L^{p,\infty}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T)}.

Similarly,

\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int^{t}_{0}|K(t^{\prime}-s)-K(t-s)||b_{s}|\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{p}\right]^{1/p}\leq\int^{t}_{0}|K(t^{\prime}-s)-K(t-s)|\,\mathrm{d}s\,\|b\|_{L^{p,\infty}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T)}.

Therefore, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\int^{t^{\prime}}_{0}K(t^{\prime}-s)b_{s}\,\mathrm{d}s-\int^{t}_{0}K(t-s)b_{s}\,\mathrm{d}s\right|^{p}\right]^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leq\left\{\int^{t^{\prime}}_{t}|K(t^{\prime}-s)|\,\mathrm{d}s+\int^{t}_{0}|K(t^{\prime}-s)-K(t-s)|\,\mathrm{d}s\right\}\|b\|_{L^{p,\infty}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T)}.

Since
K\in L^{1}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
, the last term above tends to zero as
t^{\prime}-t\to 0
. Therefore, the process
(\int^{t}_{0}K(t-s)b_{s}\,\mathrm{d}s)_{t\in[0,T]}
is in
C^{p}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n})
. The linearity of the map
b\mapsto(\int^{t}_{0}K(t-s)b_{s}\,\mathrm{d}s)_{t\in[0,T]}
is clear. Furthermore, for any
t\in[0,T]
,
\tau\in[0,T)
and
\varepsilon\in(0,T-\tau]
, noting the definition of the norm
\|\cdot\|_{1,\varepsilon}
, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\int^{t}_{0}K(t-s)b_{s}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\tau,\tau+\varepsilon]}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\right|^{p}\right]^{1/p}\leq\int^{t}_{0}|K(t-s)|\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\tau,\tau+\varepsilon]}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\|b\|_{L^{p,\infty}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T)}\leq\|K\|_{1,\varepsilon}\|b\|_{L^{p,\infty}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T)}.

Hence, the estimate (
2.3
) holds. In particular, considering
\tau=0
and
\varepsilon=T
, we see that the map
b\mapsto(\int^{t}_{0}K(t-s)b_{s}\,\mathrm{d}s)_{t\in[0,T]}
is a bounded linear operator from
L^{p,\infty}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n})
to
C^{p}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Next, we show the assertion (ii). Suppose that
K\in L^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
and
p\in[2,\infty)
. Let
\sigma\in L^{p,\infty}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n})
be fixed. For each
0\leq t\leq t^{\prime}\leq T
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\int^{t^{\prime}}_{0}K(t^{\prime}-s)\sigma_{s}\,\mathrm{d}s-\int^{t}_{0}K(t-s)\sigma_{s}\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}\right|^{p}\right]^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\int^{t^{\prime}}_{t}K(t^{\prime}-s)\sigma_{s}\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}\right|^{p}\right]^{1/p}+\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\int^{t}_{0}(K(t^{\prime}-s)-K(t-s))\sigma_{s}\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}\right|^{p}\right]^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leq C_{p}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int^{t^{\prime}}_{t}|K(t^{\prime}-s)|^{2}|\sigma_{s}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{p/2}\right]^{1/p}+C_{p}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int^{t}_{0}|K(t^{\prime}-s)-K(t-s)|^{2}|\sigma_{s}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{p/2}\right]^{1/p},

where we used the Burkholder–Davis–Gundy inequality in the last line. Using Minkowski’s integral inequality, one has

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int^{t^{\prime}}_{t}|K(t^{\prime}-s)|^{2}|\sigma_{s}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{p/2}\right]^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int^{t^{\prime}}_{t}|K(t^{\prime}-s)|^{2}\mathbb{E}\big[|\sigma_{s}|^{p}\big]^{2/p}\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int^{t^{\prime}}_{t}|K(t^{\prime}-s)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}\|\sigma\|_{L^{p,\infty}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T)}.

Similarly,

\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int^{t}_{0}|K(t^{\prime}-s)-K(t-s)|^{2}|\sigma_{s}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{p/2}\right]^{1/p}\leq\left(\int^{t}_{0}|K(t^{\prime}-s)-K(t-s)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}\|\sigma\|_{L^{p,\infty}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T)}.

Hence,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\int^{t^{\prime}}_{0}K(t^{\prime}-s)\sigma_{s}\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}-\int^{t}_{0}K(t-s)\sigma_{s}\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}\right|^{p}\right]^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leq C_{p}\left\{\left(\int^{t^{\prime}}_{t}|K(t^{\prime}-s)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}+\left(\int^{t}_{0}|K(t^{\prime}-s)-K(t-s)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}\right\}\|\sigma\|_{L^{p,\infty}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T)}.

Since
K\in L^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
, the last term above tends to zero as
t^{\prime}-t\to 0
. Therefore, the process
(\int^{t}_{0}K(t-s)\sigma_{s}\,\mathrm{d}W_{s})_{t\in[0,T]}
is in
C^{p}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n})
. The linearity of the map
\sigma\mapsto(\int^{t}_{0}K(t-s)\sigma_{s}\,\mathrm{d}W_{s})_{t\in[0,T]}
is clear. Furthermore, for any
t\in[0,T]
,
\tau\in[0,T)
and
\varepsilon\in(0,T-\tau]
, noting the definition of the norm
\|\cdot\|_{2,\varepsilon}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\int^{t}_{0}K(t-s)\sigma_{s}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\tau,\tau+\varepsilon]}(s)\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}\right|^{p}\right]^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leq C_{p}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int^{t}_{0}|K(t-s)|^{2}|\sigma_{s}|^{2}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\tau,\tau+\varepsilon]}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{p/2}\right]^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leq C_{p}\left(\int^{t}_{0}|K(t-s)|^{2}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\tau,\tau+\varepsilon]}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}\|\sigma\|_{L^{p,\infty}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T)}

\displaystyle\leq C_{p}\|K\|_{2,\varepsilon}\|\sigma\|_{L^{p,\infty}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T)}.

Hence, the estimate (
2.4
) holds. In particular, considering
\tau=0
and
\varepsilon=T
, we see that the map
\sigma\mapsto(\int^{t}_{0}K(t-s)\sigma_{s}\,\mathrm{d}W_{s})_{t\in[0,T]}
is a bounded linear operator from
L^{p,\infty}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n})
to
C^{p}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
∎

The following is a known result on the well-posedness and moment estimate for SVEs with singular kernels; see for example
[
41
]
.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
p\in[2,\infty)
. Fix
\xi\in C^{p}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
K_{b}\in L^{1}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
and
K_{\sigma}\in L^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
. Let
b,\sigma:\Omega\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
be progressively measurable maps. Assume that the processes
(b(t,0))_{t\in[0,T]}
and
(\sigma(t,0))_{t\in[0,T]}
are in
L^{p,\infty}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Assume furthermore that there exists a constant
L>0
such that

|b(t,x)-b(t,x^{\prime})|+|\sigma(t,x)-\sigma(t,x^{\prime})|\leq L|x-x^{\prime}|

for any
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
(\omega,t)\in\Omega\times[0,T]
. Then, the SVE

X_{t}=\xi_{t}+\int^{t}_{0}K_{b}(t-s)b(s,X_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s+\int^{t}_{0}K_{\sigma}(t-s)\sigma(s,X_{s})\mathrm{d}W_{s},\ t\in[0,T],

admits a unique solution
X\in C^{p}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Furthermore, there exists a constant
C_{p}>0
, which depends only on
p,T,K_{b},K_{\sigma}
and
L
, such that

\|X\|_{C^{p}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T)}\leq C_{p}\Big\{\|\xi\|_{C^{p}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T)}+\|b(\cdot,0)\|_{L^{p,\infty}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T)}+\|\sigma(\cdot,0)\|_{L^{p,\infty}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T)}\Big\}.

Proof.

The assertion follows from
[
41
, Theorem 3.1]
applying to
g(t)=\xi_{t}+\int^{t}_{0}K_{b}(t-s)b(s,0)\,\mathrm{d}s+\int^{t}_{0}K_{\sigma}(t-s)\sigma(s,0)\,\mathrm{d}s
,
A(t,s,x)=K_{b}(t-s)(b(s,x)-b(s,0))
and
B(t,s,x)=K_{\sigma}(t-s)(\sigma(s,x)-\sigma(s,0))
in its notation, together with
Lemma

2.2
.
∎

2.3
Problem formulation

We consider the optimal control problem described by the controlled SVE (
1.1
) and the cost functional (
1.2
). We impose the following assumptions on the coefficients.

Assumption 1
.

•

\xi\in\bigcap_{p\in[2,\infty)}C^{p}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
K_{b}\in L^{1}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
,
K_{\sigma}\in L^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
.

•

The control domain
U
is a separable topological space. We equip
U
with the Borel
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{B}(U)
.

•

The maps
b,\sigma:\Omega\times[0,T]\times U\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
f:\Omega\times[0,T]\times U\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
are progressively measurable, and
h:\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
is
\mathcal{F}_{T}\otimes\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
-measurable. Furthermore, assume that the following hold:

–

For any
(\omega,t,x)\in\Omega\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the maps
u\mapsto b(t,u,x),\sigma(t,u,x),f(t,u,x)
are continuous.

–

For any
(\omega,t,u)\in\Omega\times[0,T]\times U
, the maps
x\mapsto b(t,u,x),\sigma(t,u,x),f(t,u,x),h(x)
are twice continuously differentiable.

–

There exist constants
\kappa,\gamma>0
and a progressively measurable map
R:\Omega\times[0,T]\times U\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that, for any
(\omega,t,u)\in\Omega\times[0,T]\times U
and any
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

\displaystyle|\varphi(t,u,0)|\leq R(t,u),\ \ |\varphi_{x}(t,u,x)|+|\varphi_{xx}(t,u,x)|\leq\kappa,

\displaystyle|\varphi_{xx}(t,u,x)-\varphi_{xx}(t,u,x^{\prime})|\leq R(t,u)\big(1\vee|x|\vee|x^{\prime}|\big)^{\gamma}|x-x^{\prime}|,\ \ \text{for $\varphi\in\{b,\sigma\}$,}

and

\displaystyle|f(t,u,x)|+|f_{x}(t,u,x)|+|f_{xx}(t,u,x)|\leq R(t,u)\big(1\vee|x|\big)^{\gamma},

\displaystyle|f_{xx}(t,u,x)-f_{xx}(t,u,x^{\prime})|\leq R(t,u)\big(1\vee|x|\vee|x^{\prime}|\big)^{\gamma}|x-x^{\prime}|,

\displaystyle|h(x)|+|h_{x}(x)|+|h_{xx}(x)|\leq\kappa\big(1\vee|x|\big)^{\gamma},

\displaystyle|h_{xx}(x)-h_{xx}(x^{\prime})|\leq\kappa\big(1\vee|x|\vee|x^{\prime}|\big)^{\gamma}|x-x^{\prime}|.

Moreover, for each
u\in U
,

(R(t,u))_{t\in[0,T]}\in\bigcap_{p\in[2,\infty)}L^{p,\infty}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T;\mathbb{R}_{+}).

Remark 2.4
.

The separability of the control domain
U
and the continuity of
b
,
\sigma
and
f
with respect to the control variable
u\in U
are used only in the last step of the proof of the global maximum principle (
Theorem

4.15
).

Let
Assumption

1
hold. We denote by
\mathcal{U}
the set of all
U
-valued progressively measurable processes
u=(u_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
such that

R^{u}\in\bigcap_{p\in[2,\infty)}L^{p,\infty}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T;\mathbb{R}_{+}),\ \ \text{where $R^{u}_{t}:=R(t,u_{t})$, $t\in[0,T]$}.

Each element
u\in\mathcal{U}
is referred to as a control process. By the assumption, every constant process
u_{t}=u_{0}\in U
,
t\in[0,T]
, belongs to
\mathcal{U}
. Also, for any
u,v\in\mathcal{U}
and any progressively measurable set
A\subset\Omega\times[0,T]
, we have
u\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{A}+v\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{(\Omega\times[0,T])\setminus A}\in\mathcal{U}
and
R^{u\scriptsize{\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.04861pt\mbox{\rm{l}}}_{A}+v\scriptsize{\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.04861pt\mbox{\rm{l}}}_{(\Omega\times[0,T])\setminus A}}\leq R^{u}\vee R^{v}
. By
Lemma

2.3
, for any
u\in\mathcal{U}
, there exists a unique solution
X^{u}\in\bigcap_{p\in[2,\infty)}C^{p}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n})
to the controlled SVE (
1.1
), which we call the state process corresponding to
u
. Furthermore, the following estimate holds:

\|X^{u}\|_{C^{p}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T)}\leq C_{p}\Big\{\|\xi\|_{C^{p}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T)}+\|R^{u}\|_{L^{p,\infty}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T)}\Big\}<\infty\ \ \text{for any $p\in[2,\infty)$,}

(2.5)

where
C_{p}>0
is a constant depending only on
p,T,K_{b},K_{\sigma}
and
\kappa
. From this, we see that the cost functional
J(u)
given by (
1.2
) is well-defined and finite. We say that a control process
\hat{u}\in\mathcal{U}
is an (open-loop)
optimal control
of the stochastic Volterra control problem (
1.1
)–(
1.2
) if it holds that

J(\hat{u})=\inf_{u\in\mathcal{U}}J(u).

The purpose of this paper is to derive a
global maximum principle
which provides a necessary condition for a given control process
\hat{u}\in\mathcal{U}
to be optimal.

3
Taylor expansion of the controlled SVE with respect to the spike variation

In order to derive the global maximum principle, we first investigate the Taylor type expansion for the controlled SVE (
1.1
) with respect to the spike variation. Let
Assumption

1
hold. Fix a control process
\hat{u}\in\mathcal{U}
, and denote the corresponding state process by
\hat{X}:=X^{\hat{u}}
. The control process
\hat{u}
is a candidate of optimal controls, but we do not assume the optimality for a while. We denote
\hat{R}:=R^{\hat{u}}
, and

\displaystyle\hat{\varphi}_{x}(t):=\varphi_{x}(t,\hat{u}_{t},\hat{X}_{t})\ \ \text{and}\ \ \hat{\varphi}_{xx}(t):=\varphi_{xx}(t,\hat{u}_{t},\hat{X}_{t})\ \ \text{for $\varphi\in\{b,\sigma,f\}$;}

\displaystyle\hat{h}_{x}:=h_{x}(\hat{X}_{T})\ \ \text{and}\ \ \hat{h}_{xx}:=h_{xx}(\hat{X}_{T}).

Furthermore, given another control process
v\in\mathcal{U}
, we denote

\displaystyle\delta\varphi^{v}(t):=\varphi(t,v_{t},\hat{X}_{t})-\varphi(t,\hat{u}_{t},\hat{X}_{t}),

\displaystyle\delta\varphi^{v}_{x}(t):=\varphi_{x}(t,v_{t},\hat{X}_{t})-\varphi_{x}(t,\hat{u}_{t},\hat{X}_{t}),

\displaystyle\delta\varphi^{v}_{xx}(t):=\varphi_{xx}(t,v_{t},\hat{X}_{t})-\varphi_{xx}(t,\hat{u}_{t},\hat{X}_{t}),

for
\varphi\in\{b,\sigma,f\}
. By
Assumption

1
and the estimate (
2.5
), we see that

\begin{split}&|\hat{\varphi}_{x}|+|\hat{\varphi}_{xx}|\leq\kappa,\ \ |\delta\varphi^{v}_{x}|+|\delta\varphi^{v}_{xx}|\leq 2\kappa,\\
&|\delta\varphi^{v}|\leq R^{v}+\hat{R}+2\kappa|\hat{X}|\in\bigcap_{p\in[2,\infty)}L^{p,\infty}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T;\mathbb{R}_{+}),\ \ \text{for $\varphi\in\{b,\sigma\}$,}\end{split}

(3.1)

and

\begin{split}&|\hat{f}_{x}|+|\hat{f}_{xx}|\leq\hat{R}\big(1\vee|\hat{X}|\big)^{\gamma}\in\bigcap_{p\in[2,\infty)}L^{p,\infty}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T;\mathbb{R}_{+}),\\
&|\delta f^{v}|+|\delta f^{v}_{x}|+|\delta f^{v}_{xx}|\leq\big(R^{v}+\hat{R}\big)\big(1\vee|\hat{X}|\big)^{\gamma}\in\bigcap_{p\in[2,\infty)}L^{p,\infty}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T;\mathbb{R}_{+}),\\
&|\hat{h}_{x}|+|\hat{h}_{xx}|\leq\kappa\big(1\vee|\hat{X}_{T}|\big)^{\gamma}\in\bigcap_{p\in[2,\infty)}L^{p}_{\mathcal{F}_{T}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}_{+}).\end{split}

(3.2)

For each
v\in\mathcal{U}
,
\tau\in[0,T)
and
\varepsilon\in(0,T-\tau]
, define the
spike variation

u^{v,\tau,\varepsilon}
of
\hat{u}
with respect to
(v,\tau,\varepsilon)
by

u^{v,\tau,\varepsilon}_{t}:=\hat{u}_{t}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[0,T]\setminus[\tau,\tau+\varepsilon]}(t)+v_{t}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\tau,\tau+\varepsilon]}(t),\ \ t\in[0,T].

(3.3)

Notice that
u^{v,\tau,\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{U}
. Let
X^{v,\tau,\varepsilon}:=X^{u^{v,\tau,\varepsilon}}
be the state process corresponding to the control process
u^{v,\tau,\varepsilon}
. We investigate a Taylor type expansion of
X^{v,\tau,\varepsilon}
around
\hat{X}
in the spirit of
[
27
]
. For such a purpose, we introduce the following
first order variational SVE
:

\begin{split}X^{1,v,\tau,\varepsilon}_{t}&=\int^{t}_{0}K_{b}(t-s)\Big\{\hat{b}_{x}(s)X^{1,v,\tau,\varepsilon}_{s}+\delta b^{v}(s)\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\tau,\tau+\varepsilon]}(s)\Big\}\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt+\int^{t}_{0}K_{\sigma}(t-s)\Big\{\hat{\sigma}_{x}(s)X^{1,v,\tau,\varepsilon}_{s}+\delta\sigma^{v}(s)\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\tau,\tau+\varepsilon]}(s)\Big\}\,\mathrm{d}W_{s},\ \ t\in[0,T],\end{split}

(3.4)

and the following
second order variational SVE
:

\begin{split}X^{2,v,\tau,\varepsilon}_{t}&=\int^{t}_{0}K_{b}(t-s)\Big\{\hat{b}_{x}(s)X^{2,v,\tau,\varepsilon}_{s}+\frac{1}{2}\big\langle\hat{b}_{xx}(s)X^{1,v,\tau,\varepsilon}_{s},X^{1,v,\tau,\varepsilon}_{s}\big\rangle+\delta b^{v}_{x}(s)X^{1,v,\tau,\varepsilon}_{s}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\tau,\tau+\varepsilon]}(s)\Big\}\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt+\int^{t}_{0}K_{\sigma}(t-s)\Big\{\hat{\sigma}_{x}(s)X^{2,v,\tau,\varepsilon}_{s}+\frac{1}{2}\big\langle\hat{\sigma}_{xx}(s)X^{1,v,\tau,\varepsilon}_{s},X^{1,v,\tau,\varepsilon}_{s}\big\rangle+\delta\sigma^{v}_{x}(s)X^{1,v,\tau,\varepsilon}_{s}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\tau,\tau+\varepsilon]}(s)\Big\}\,\mathrm{d}W_{s},\\
&\hskip 170.71652ptt\in[0,T].\end{split}

(3.5)

Here, recall the notations (
2.1
) and (
2.2
). Noting (
3.1
), by
Lemma

2.3
, there exist unique solutions
X^{1,v,\tau,\varepsilon},X^{2,v,\tau,\varepsilon}\in\bigcap_{p\in[2,\infty)}C^{p}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n})
to the above SVEs. Also, notice that
X^{1,v,\tau,\varepsilon}_{t}=X^{2,v,\tau,\varepsilon}_{t}=0
a.s. for any
t\in[0,\tau]
. We denote
X^{1,2,v,\tau,\varepsilon}:=X^{1,v,\tau,\varepsilon}+X^{2,v,\tau,\varepsilon}
. Then
X^{1,2,v,\tau,\varepsilon}
solves the following SVE:

\begin{split}X^{1,2,v,\tau,\varepsilon}_{t}&=\int^{t}_{0}K_{b}(t-s)\Big\{\hat{b}_{x}(s)X^{1,2,v,\tau,\varepsilon}_{s}+\frac{1}{2}\big\langle\hat{b}_{xx}(s)X^{1,v,\tau,\varepsilon}_{s},X^{1,v,\tau,\varepsilon}_{s}\big\rangle\\
&\hskip 128.0374pt+\Big(\delta b^{v}(s)+\delta b^{v}_{x}(s)X^{1,v,\tau,\varepsilon}_{s}\Big)\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\tau,\tau+\varepsilon]}(s)\Big\}\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt+\int^{t}_{0}K_{\sigma}(t-s)\Big\{\hat{\sigma}_{x}(s)X^{1,2,v,\tau,\varepsilon}_{s}+\frac{1}{2}\big\langle\hat{\sigma}_{xx}(s)X^{1,v,\tau,\varepsilon}_{s},X^{1,v,\tau,\varepsilon}_{s}\big\rangle\\
&\hskip 142.26378pt+\Big(\delta\sigma^{v}(s)+\delta\sigma^{v}_{x}(s)X^{1,v,\tau,\varepsilon}_{s}\Big)\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\tau,\tau+\varepsilon]}(s)\Big\}\,\mathrm{d}W_{s},\ \ t\in[0,T].\end{split}

(3.6)

Define

\displaystyle\delta X^{v,\tau,\varepsilon}:=X^{v,\tau,\varepsilon}-\hat{X},

\displaystyle\delta X^{1,v,\tau,\varepsilon}:=X^{v,\tau,\varepsilon}-\hat{X}-X^{1,v,\tau,\varepsilon}=\delta X^{v,\tau,\varepsilon}-X^{1,v,\tau,\varepsilon},

\displaystyle\delta X^{1,2,v,\tau,\varepsilon}:=X^{v,\tau,\varepsilon}-\hat{X}-X^{1,v,\tau,\varepsilon}-X^{2,v,\tau,\varepsilon}=\delta X^{v,\tau,\varepsilon}-X^{1,2,v,\tau,\varepsilon}=\delta X^{1,v,\tau,\varepsilon}-X^{2,v,\tau,\varepsilon}.

Also, we set

\begin{split}J^{1,2,v,\tau,\varepsilon}&:=\mathbb{E}\left[\hat{h}_{x}X^{1,2,v,\tau,\varepsilon}_{T}+\frac{1}{2}\big\langle\hat{h}_{xx}X^{1,v,\tau,\varepsilon}_{T},X^{1,v,\tau,\varepsilon}_{T}\big\rangle\right]\\
&\hskip 28.45274pt+\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{0}\Big\{\hat{f}_{x}(s)X^{1,2,v,\tau,\varepsilon}_{s}+\frac{1}{2}\big\langle\hat{f}_{xx}(s)X^{1,v,\tau,\varepsilon}_{s},X^{1,v,\tau,\varepsilon}_{s}\big\rangle\Big\}\,\mathrm{d}s\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{\tau+\varepsilon}_{\tau}\delta f^{v}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\right].\end{split}

(3.7)

Noting (
3.2
) and
X^{1},X^{1,2}\in\bigcap_{p\in[2,\infty)}C^{p}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n})
, the right-hand side above is well-defined and finite. Finally, we set

\delta J^{1,2,v,\tau,\varepsilon}:=J(u^{v,\tau,\varepsilon})-J(\hat{u})-J^{1,2,v,\tau,\varepsilon}.

Under the above setting, the following important proposition holds.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
Assumption

1
hold. Fix
\hat{u},v\in\mathcal{U}
. Then, for any
p\in[2,\infty)
, there exists a constant
C_{p}>0
such that, for any
\tau\in[0,T)
and
\varepsilon\in(0,T-\tau]
, the following hold:

\displaystyle\|\delta X^{v,\tau,\varepsilon}\|_{C^{p}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T)}\leq C_{p}\big\{\|K_{b}\|_{1,\varepsilon}+\|K_{\sigma}\|_{2,\varepsilon}\big\},

(3.8)

\displaystyle\|X^{1,v,\tau,\varepsilon}\|_{C^{p}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T)}\leq C_{p}\big\{\|K_{b}\|_{1,\varepsilon}+\|K_{\sigma}\|_{2,\varepsilon}\big\},

(3.9)

\displaystyle\|\delta X^{1,v,\tau,\varepsilon}\|_{C^{p}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T)}\leq C_{p}\big\{\|K_{b}\|_{1,\varepsilon}+\|K_{\sigma}\|_{2,\varepsilon}\big\}^{2},

(3.10)

\displaystyle\|X^{2,v,\tau,\varepsilon}\|_{C^{p}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T)}\leq C_{p}\big\{\|K_{b}\|_{1,\varepsilon}+\|K_{\sigma}\|_{2,\varepsilon}\big\}^{2},

(3.11)

\displaystyle\|\delta X^{1,2,v,\tau,\varepsilon}\|_{C^{p}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T)}\leq C_{p}\big\{\|K_{b}\|_{1,\varepsilon}+\|K_{\sigma}\|_{2,\varepsilon}\big\}^{3}.

(3.12)

Furthermore, there exists a constant
C>0
such that, for any
\tau\in[0,T)
and
\varepsilon\in(0,T-\tau]
,

|\delta J^{1,2,v,\tau,\varepsilon}|\leq C\big\{\|K_{b}\|_{1,\varepsilon}+\|K_{\sigma}\|_{2,\varepsilon}\big\}^{3}+C\varepsilon\big\{\|K_{b}\|_{1,\varepsilon}+\|K_{\sigma}\|_{2,\varepsilon}\big\}.

(3.13)

In particular, if
K_{b}\in L^{3/2}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
and
K_{\sigma}\in L^{6}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
, then

|\delta J^{1,2,v,\tau,\varepsilon}|=o(\varepsilon)\ \ \text{as $\varepsilon\downarrow 0$ for any $\tau\in[0,T)$}.

(3.14)

Proof.

In this proof, we omit the super scripts
v,\tau,\varepsilon
. For example, we denote
u=u^{v,\tau,\varepsilon}
,
X=X^{v,\tau,\varepsilon}
,
X^{1}=X^{1,v,\tau,\varepsilon}
,
\delta b=\delta b^{v}
,
R^{v}=R
, and so on. For each
p\in[2,\infty)
, we denote by
C_{p}
a positive constant which depends on
p
,
\hat{u}
,
v
and other parameters given in
Assumption

1
but not on
\tau
or
\varepsilon
. The value of
C_{p}
may vary from line to line. Notice that, by (
2.5
) and
R^{u}\leq R\vee\hat{R}
,

\|\hat{X}\|_{C^{p}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T)}+\|X\|_{C^{p}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T)}\leq C_{p}\ \ \text{for any $p\in[2,\infty)$.}

(3.15)

For
\varphi\in\{b,\sigma\}
, define

\tilde{\varphi}_{x}(s):=\int^{1}_{0}\varphi_{x}(s,u_{s},r\hat{X}_{s}+(1-r)X_{s})\,\mathrm{d}r,\ \tilde{\varphi}_{xx}(s):=2\int^{1}_{0}r\varphi_{xx}(s,u_{s},r\hat{X}_{s}+(1-r)X_{s})\,\mathrm{d}r.

Then, by Taylor’s formula, we have

\displaystyle\varphi(s,u_{s},X_{s})-\varphi(s,\hat{u}_{s},\hat{X}_{s})

\displaystyle=\tilde{\varphi}_{x}(s)\delta X_{s}+\delta\varphi(s)\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\tau,\tau+\varepsilon]}(s)

(3.16)

\displaystyle=\hat{\varphi}_{x}(s)\delta X_{s}+\Big(\tilde{\varphi}_{x}(s)-\varphi_{x}(s,u_{s},\hat{X}_{s})\Big)\delta X_{s}+\Big(\delta\varphi(s)+\delta\varphi_{x}(s)\delta X_{s}\Big)\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\tau,\tau+\varepsilon]}(s)

(3.17)

\displaystyle=\hat{\varphi}_{x}(s)\delta X_{s}+\frac{1}{2}\big\langle\hat{\varphi}_{xx}(s)\delta X_{s},\delta X_{s}\big\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\Big\langle\Big(\tilde{\varphi}_{xx}(s)-\varphi_{xx}(s,u_{s},\hat{X}_{s})\Big)\delta X_{s},\delta X_{s}\Big\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\Big(\delta\varphi(s)+\delta\varphi_{x}(s)\delta X_{s}+\frac{1}{2}\big\langle\delta\varphi_{xx}(s)\delta X_{s},\delta X_{s}\big\rangle\Big)\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\tau,\tau+\varepsilon]}(s).

(3.18)

The estimate (
3.8
) for
\delta X=\delta X^{v,\tau,\varepsilon}
.

By the expansion (
3.16
), we see that
\delta X
solves the SVE

\delta X_{t}=\delta\xi_{t}+\int^{t}_{0}K_{b}(t-s)\tilde{b}_{x}(s)\delta X_{s}\,\mathrm{d}s+\int^{t}_{0}K_{\sigma}(t-s)\tilde{\sigma}_{x}(s)\delta X_{s}\,\mathrm{d}W_{s},\ \ t\in[0,T],

where

\delta\xi_{t}:=\int^{t}_{0}K_{b}(t-s)\delta b(s)\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\tau,\tau+\varepsilon]}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s+\int^{t}_{0}K_{\sigma}(t-s)\delta\sigma(s)\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\tau,\tau+\varepsilon]}(s)\,\mathrm{d}W_{s},\ \ t\in[0,T].

Noting (
3.1
), by
Lemma

2.2
, we see that
\delta\xi\in C^{p}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n})
for any
p\in[2,\infty)
and

\|\delta\xi\|_{C^{p}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T)}\leq C_{p}\big\{\|K_{b}\|_{1,\varepsilon}+\|K_{\sigma}\|_{2,\varepsilon}\big\}.

Then, noting that
|\tilde{b}_{x}(s)|\leq\kappa
and
|\tilde{\sigma}_{x}(s)|\leq\kappa
, by
Lemma

2.3
, we get the estimate (
3.8
).

The estimate (
3.9
) for
X^{1}=X^{1,v,\tau,\varepsilon}
.

Notice that the first order variational SVE (

) for
X^{1}
can be written as

X^{1}_{t}=\xi^{1}_{t}+\int^{t}_{0}K_{b}(t-s)\hat{b}_{x}(s)X^{1}_{s}\,\mathrm{d}s+\int^{t}_{0}K_{\sigma}(t-s)\hat{\sigma}_{x}(s)X^{1}_{s}\,\mathrm{d}W_{s},\ \ t\in[0,T],

where

\xi^{1}_{t}:=\int^{t}_{0}K_{b}(t-s)\delta b(s)\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\tau,\tau+\varepsilon]}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s+\int^{t}_{0}K_{\sigma}(t-s)\delta\sigma(s)\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\tau,\tau+\varepsilon]}(s)\,\mathrm{d}W_{s},\ \ t\in[0,T].

Noting (
3.1
), by
Lemma

2.2
, we see that
\xi^{1}\in C^{p}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n})
for any
p\in[2,\infty)
and

\|\xi^{1}\|_{C^{p}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T)}\leq C_{p}\big\{\|K_{b}\|_{1,\varepsilon}+\|K_{\sigma}\|_{2,\varepsilon}\big\}.

Then, noting that
|\hat{b}_{x}(s)|\leq\kappa
and
|\hat{\sigma}_{x}(s)|\leq\kappa
, by

, we get the estimate (

).

The estimate (
3.10
) for
\delta X^{1}=\delta X^{1,v,\tau,\varepsilon}
.

By the expansion (
3.17
) and the first order variational SVE (
3.4
) for
X^{1}
, we see that
\delta X^{1}
solves the SVE

\delta X^{1}_{t}=\delta\xi^{1}_{t}+\int^{t}_{0}K_{b}(t-s)\hat{b}_{x}(s)\delta X^{1}_{s}\,\mathrm{d}s+\int^{t}_{0}K_{\sigma}(t-s)\hat{\sigma}_{x}(s)\delta X^{1}_{s}\,\mathrm{d}W_{s},\ \ t\in[0,T],

where

\displaystyle\delta\xi^{1}_{t}

\displaystyle:=\int^{t}_{0}K_{b}(t-s)\Big(\tilde{b}_{x}(s)-b_{x}(s,u_{s},\hat{X}_{s})\Big)\delta X_{s}\,\mathrm{d}s+\int^{t}_{0}K_{b}(t-s)\delta b_{x}(s)\delta X_{s}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\tau,\tau+\varepsilon]}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\int^{t}_{0}K_{\sigma}(t-s)\Big(\tilde{\sigma}_{x}(s)-\sigma_{x}(s,u_{s},\hat{X}_{s})\Big)\delta X_{s}\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}+\int^{t}_{0}K_{\sigma}(t-s)\delta\sigma_{x}(s)\delta X_{s}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\tau,\tau+\varepsilon]}(s)\,\mathrm{d}W_{s},\ \ t\in[0,T].

By
Assumption

1
, we have
|\tilde{\varphi}_{x}(s)-\varphi_{x}(s,u_{s},\hat{X}_{s})|\leq\frac{\kappa}{2}|\delta X_{s}|
for
\varphi\in\{b,\sigma\}
. From this and (
3.1
), together with the estimate (
3.8
) for
\delta X
, by using
Lemma

2.2
, we see that
\delta\xi^{1}\in C^{p}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n})
for any

and

\displaystyle\|\delta\xi^{1}\|_{C^{p}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T)}

\displaystyle\leq C_{p}\big\{\|K_{b}\|_{1,\varepsilon}+\|K_{\sigma}\|_{2,\varepsilon}\big\}^{2}+C_{p}\|K_{b}\|_{1,\varepsilon}\big\{\|K_{b}\|_{1,\varepsilon}+\|K_{\sigma}\|_{2,\varepsilon}\big\}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+C_{p}\big\{\|K_{b}\|_{1,\varepsilon}+\|K_{\sigma}\|_{2,\varepsilon}\big\}^{2}+C_{p}\|K_{\sigma}\|_{2,\varepsilon}\big\{\|K_{b}\|_{1,\varepsilon}+\|K_{\sigma}\|_{2,\varepsilon}\big\}

\displaystyle\leq C_{p}\big\{\|K_{b}\|_{1,\varepsilon}+\|K_{\sigma}\|_{2,\varepsilon}\big\}^{2}.

Then, by
Lemma

2.3
, we get the estimate (
3.10
).

The estimate (

) for

.

Notice that the second order variational SVE (

) for

can be written as

X^{2}_{t}=\xi^{2}_{t}+\int^{t}_{0}K_{b}(t-s)\hat{b}_{x}(s)X^{2}_{s}\,\mathrm{d}s+\int^{t}_{0}K_{\sigma}(t-s)\hat{\sigma}_{x}(s)X^{2}_{s}\,\mathrm{d}W_{s},\ \ t\in[0,T],

where

\displaystyle\xi^{2}_{t}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\int^{t}_{0}K_{b}(t-s)\big\langle\hat{b}_{xx}(s)X^{1}_{s},X^{1}_{s}\big\rangle\,\mathrm{d}s+\int^{t}_{0}K_{b}(t-s)\delta b_{x}(s)X^{1}_{s}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\tau,\tau+\varepsilon]}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\frac{1}{2}\int^{t}_{0}K_{\sigma}(t-s)\big\langle\hat{\sigma}_{xx}(s)X^{1}_{s},X^{1}_{s}\big\rangle\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}+\int^{t}_{0}K_{\sigma}(t-s)\delta\sigma_{x}(s)X^{1}_{s}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\tau,\tau+\varepsilon]}(s)\,\mathrm{d}W_{s},\ \ t\in[0,T].

From (
3.1
), together with the estimate (
3.9
) for

, by using

, we see that
\xi^{2}\in C^{p}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n})
for any

and

\displaystyle\|\xi^{2}\|_{C^{p}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T)}

\displaystyle\leq C_{p}\big\{\|K_{b}\|_{1,\varepsilon}+\|K_{\sigma}\|_{2,\varepsilon}\big\}^{2}+C_{p}\|K_{b}\|_{1,\varepsilon}\big\{\|K_{b}\|_{1,\varepsilon}+\|K_{\sigma}\|_{2,\varepsilon}\big\}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+C_{p}\big\{\|K_{b}\|_{1,\varepsilon}+\|K_{\sigma}\|_{2,\varepsilon}\big\}^{2}+C_{p}\|K_{\sigma}\|_{2,\varepsilon}\big\{\|K_{b}\|_{1,\varepsilon}+\|K_{\sigma}\|_{2,\varepsilon}\big\}

\displaystyle\leq C_{p}\big\{\|K_{b}\|_{1,\varepsilon}+\|K_{\sigma}\|_{2,\varepsilon}\big\}^{2}.

Then, by
Lemma

2.3
, we get the estimate (
3.11
).

The estimate (
3.12
) for
\delta X^{1,2}=\delta X^{1,2,v,\tau,\varepsilon}
.

By the expansion (
3.18
) and the variational SVE (
3.6
) for
X^{1,2}=X^{1}+X^{2}
, we see that
\delta X^{1,2}
solves the SVE

\delta X^{1,2}_{t}=\delta\xi^{1,2}_{t}+\int^{t}_{0}K_{b}(t-s)\hat{b}_{x}(s)\delta X^{1,2}_{s}\,\mathrm{d}s+\int^{t}_{0}K_{\sigma}(t-s)\hat{\sigma}_{x}(s)\delta X^{1,2}_{s}\,\mathrm{d}W_{s},\ \ t\in[0,T],

where

\displaystyle\delta\xi^{1,2}_{t}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\int^{t}_{0}K_{b}(t-s)\big\langle\hat{b}_{xx}(s)\delta X^{1}_{s},\delta X_{s}+X^{1}_{s}\big\rangle\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\frac{1}{2}\int^{t}_{0}K_{b}(t-s)\Big\langle\Big(\tilde{b}_{xx}(s)-b_{xx}(s,u_{s},\hat{X}_{s})\Big)\delta X_{s},\delta X_{s}\Big\rangle\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\int^{t}_{0}K_{b}(t-s)\delta b_{x}(s)\delta X^{1}_{s}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\tau,\tau+\varepsilon]}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\frac{1}{2}\int^{t}_{0}K_{b}(t-s)\big\langle\delta b_{xx}(s)\delta X_{s},\delta X_{s}\big\rangle\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\tau,\tau+\varepsilon]}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\frac{1}{2}\int^{t}_{0}K_{\sigma}(t-s)\big\langle\hat{\sigma}_{xx}(s)\delta X^{1}_{s},\delta X_{s}+X^{1}_{s}\big\rangle\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\frac{1}{2}\int^{t}_{0}K_{\sigma}(t-s)\Big\langle\Big(\tilde{\sigma}_{xx}(s)-\sigma_{xx}(s,u_{s},\hat{X}_{s})\Big)\delta X_{s},\delta X_{s}\Big\rangle\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\int^{t}_{0}K_{\sigma}(t-s)\delta\sigma_{x}(s)\delta X^{1}_{s}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\tau,\tau+\varepsilon]}(s)\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\frac{1}{2}\int^{t}_{0}K_{\sigma}(t-s)\big\langle\delta\sigma_{xx}(s)\delta X_{s},\delta X_{s}\big\rangle\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\tau,\tau+\varepsilon]}(s)\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}

\displaystyle=:I_{1}(t)+I_{2}(t)+I_{3}(t)+I_{4}(t)+I_{5}(t)+I_{6}(t)+I_{7}(t)+I_{8}(t),\ \ t\in[0,T].

By
Assumption

1
and
R(s,u_{s})=R^{u}_{s}\leq R_{s}\vee\hat{R}_{s}
with
R=R^{v}
and
\hat{R}=R^{\hat{u}}
, we have

|\tilde{\varphi}_{xx}(s)-\varphi_{xx}(s,u_{s},\hat{X}_{s})|\leq\frac{1}{3}\big(R_{s}\vee\hat{R}_{s}\big)\big(1\vee|X_{s}|\vee|\hat{X}_{s}|\big)^{\gamma}|\delta X_{s}|\ \ \text{for $\varphi\in\{b,\sigma\}$.}

Noting (
3.1
) and (
3.15
), by using
Lemma

2.2
and Hölder’s inequality, together with the estimates (
3.8
), (
3.9
) and (
3.10
) for
\delta X
,
X^{1}
and
\delta X^{1}
, we see that
I_{k}\in C^{p}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n})
for any
p\in[2,\infty)
and
k\in\{1,\dots,8\}
, and

\|I_{k}\|_{C^{p}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T)}\leq\begin{cases}C_{p}\big\{\|K_{b}\|_{1,\varepsilon}+\|K_{\sigma}\|_{2,\varepsilon}\big\}^{3},\ \ k\in\{1,2,5,6\},\\
C_{p}\|K_{b}\|_{1,\varepsilon}\big\{\|K_{b}\|_{1,\varepsilon}+\|K_{\sigma}\|_{2,\varepsilon}\big\}^{2},\ \ k\in\{3,4\},\\
C_{p}\|K_{\sigma}\|_{2,\varepsilon}\big\{\|K_{b}\|_{1,\varepsilon}+\|K_{\sigma}\|_{2,\varepsilon}\big\}^{2},\ \ k\in\{7,8\}.\end{cases}

Hence, we see that
\delta\xi^{1,2}\in C^{p}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n})
and

\|\delta\xi^{1,2}\|_{C^{p}_{\mathbb{F}}(0,T)}\leq C_{p}\big\{\|K_{b}\|_{1,\varepsilon}+\|K_{\sigma}\|_{2,\varepsilon}\big\}^{3}.

Then, by
Lemma

2.3
, we get the estimate (
3.12
).

The estimate (
3.13
) for
\delta J^{1,2}=\delta J^{1,2,v,\tau,\varepsilon}
.

By using Taylor expansions for
h
and
f
similar to (
3.18
), we have

\displaystyle J(u)-J(\hat{u})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\hat{h}_{x}\delta X_{T}+\frac{1}{2}\big\langle\hat{h}_{xx}\delta X_{T},\delta X_{T}\big\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\Big\langle\Big(\tilde{h}_{xx}-\hat{h}_{xx}\Big)\delta X_{T},\delta X_{T}\Big\rangle\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{0}\Big\{\hat{f}_{x}(s)\delta X_{s}+\frac{1}{2}\big\langle\hat{f}_{xx}(s)\delta X_{s},\delta X_{s}\big\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\Big\langle\Big(\tilde{f}_{xx}(s)-f_{xx}(s,u_{s},\hat{X}_{s})\Big)\delta X_{s},\delta X_{s}\Big\rangle\Big\}\,\mathrm{d}s\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{\tau+\varepsilon}_{\tau}\Big\{\delta f(s)+\delta f_{x}(s)\delta X_{s}+\frac{1}{2}\big\langle\delta f_{xx}(s)\delta X_{s},\delta X_{s}\big\rangle\Big\}\,\mathrm{d}s\right],

where

\tilde{h}_{xx}:=2\int^{1}_{0}rh_{xx}(r\hat{X}_{T}+(1-r)X_{T})\,\mathrm{d}r,\ \ \tilde{f}_{xx}(s):=2\int^{1}_{0}rf_{xx}(s,u_{s},r\hat{X}_{s}+(1-r)X_{s})\,\mathrm{d}r.

Hence, by the definition (
3.7
) of
J^{1,2}=J^{1,2,v,\tau,\varepsilon}
, the term
\delta J^{1,2}=J(u)-J(\hat{u})-J^{1,2}
is expressed as

\displaystyle\delta J^{1,2}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\hat{h}_{x}\delta X^{1,2}_{T}+\frac{1}{2}\big\langle\hat{h}_{xx}\delta X^{1}_{T},\delta X_{T}+X^{1}_{T}\big\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\Big\langle\Big(\tilde{h}_{xx}-\hat{h}_{xx}\Big)\delta X_{T},\delta X_{T}\Big\rangle\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{0}\Big\{\hat{f}_{x}(s)\delta X^{1,2}_{s}+\frac{1}{2}\big\langle\hat{f}_{xx}(s)\delta X^{1}_{s},\delta X_{s}+X^{1}_{s}\big\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\Big\langle\Big(\tilde{f}_{xx}(s)-f_{xx}(s,u_{s},\hat{X}_{s})\Big)\delta X_{s},\delta X_{s}\Big\rangle\Big\}\,\mathrm{d}s\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{\tau+\varepsilon}_{\tau}\Big\{\delta f_{x}(s)\delta X_{s}+\frac{1}{2}\big\langle\delta f_{xx}(s)\delta X_{s},\delta X_{s}\big\rangle\Big\}\,\mathrm{d}s\right].

By
Assumption

1
and
R(s,u_{s})=R^{u}_{s}\leq R_{s}\vee\hat{R}_{s}
with
R=R^{v}
and
\hat{R}=R^{\hat{u}}
, we have

|\tilde{f}_{xx}(s)-f_{xx}(s,u_{s},\hat{X}_{s})|\leq\frac{1}{3}\big(R_{s}\vee\hat{R}_{s}\big)\big(1\vee|X_{s}|\vee|\hat{X}_{s}|\big)^{\gamma}|\delta X_{s}|\ \ \text{and}\ \ |\tilde{h}_{xx}-\hat{h}_{xx}|\leq\frac{\kappa}{3}(1\vee|X_{T}|\vee|\hat{X}_{T}|)^{\gamma}|\delta X_{T}|.

Noting (
3.2
) and (
3.15
), by using Hölder’s inequality and the estimates (
3.8
), (
3.9
), (
3.10
) and (
3.12
) for
\delta X
,
X^{1}
,
\delta X^{1}
and
\delta X^{1,2}
, we obtain (
3.13
). Finally, noting
Remark

2.1
, if
K_{b}\in L^{3/2}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
and
K_{\sigma}\in L^{6}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
, we see that

\|K_{b}\|^{3}_{1,\varepsilon}\leq\|K_{b}\|^{3}_{3/2,\varepsilon}\varepsilon,\ \ \|K_{\sigma}\|^{3}_{2,\varepsilon}\leq\|K_{\sigma}\|^{3}_{6,\varepsilon}\varepsilon,

and

\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\|K_{b}\|_{1,\varepsilon}=\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\|K_{b}\|_{3/2,\varepsilon}=\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\|K_{\sigma}\|_{2,\varepsilon}=\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\|K_{\sigma}\|_{6,\varepsilon}=0.

From the above estimates and (
3.13
), we get (
3.14
). This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 3.2
.

Consider for example the fractional kernels
K_{b}(t)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\beta_{b})}t^{\beta_{b}-1}I_{n\times n}
and
K_{\sigma}(t)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\beta_{\sigma})}t^{\beta_{\sigma}-1}
with
\beta_{b}\in(0,1)
and
\beta_{\sigma}\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
. For these kernels, the conditions
K_{b}\in L^{3/2}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
and
K_{\sigma}\in L^{6}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
hold if and only if
\beta_{b}>\frac{1}{3}
and
\beta_{\sigma}>\frac{5}{6}
.

4
Adjoint equations and global maximum principle

By the variational argument (
Proposition

3.1
), under suitable integrability conditions on the kernels, we immediately obtain a first order necessary condition for a fixed control
\hat{u}\in\mathcal{U}
to be optimal, namely,

\liminf_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}J^{1,2,v,\tau,\varepsilon}\geq 0

for any
v\in\mathcal{U}
and
\tau\in[0,T)
, where
J^{1,2,v,\tau,\varepsilon}
is defined by (
3.7
). However, the above is still an implicit necessary condition that is not easily applicable, since the term
J^{1,2,v,\tau,\varepsilon}
involves the solutions
X^{1,v,\tau,\varepsilon}
and
X^{1,2,v,\tau,\varepsilon}
to the variational SVEs (
3.4
) and (
3.6
), respectively, which depend on the choices of
v,\tau,\varepsilon
. Our aim is to derive first and second order adjoint equations, which enable us to get rid of the terms
X^{1,v,\tau,\varepsilon}
and
X^{1,2,v,\tau,\varepsilon}
from
J^{1,2,v,\tau,\varepsilon}
through a kind of duality relation. To do so, we need to treat the quadratic functional of
(X^{1,v,\tau,\varepsilon},X^{1,2,v,\tau,\varepsilon})
appearing in the expression (
3.7
) of
J^{1,2,v,\tau,\varepsilon}
. One of the main difficulty here is that the solutions
X^{1,v,\tau,\varepsilon}
,
X^{1,2,v,\tau,\varepsilon}
of variational SVEs (
3.4
) and (
3.6
) with singular kernels are no longer semimartingales. The lack of the semimartingale property prevents us to use Itô’s formula and the standard BSDE technique, which have been used in the study of stochastic control problems for SDEs.

In order to overcome the difficulty mentioned above, we utilize (and slightly generalize) the infinite dimensional framework proposed in
[
13
]
. We impose the following structure condition on the kernels.

Assumption 2
.

The kernels
K_{b},K_{\sigma}:(0,T)\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
are of the following forms:

K_{b}(t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}e^{-\theta t}M_{b}(\theta)\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\ \ \text{and}\ \ K_{\sigma}(t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}e^{-\theta t}M_{\sigma}(\theta)\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta),\ \ t\in(0,T),

(4.1)

for some Borel measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
and matrix-valued Borel measurable maps
M_{b},M_{\sigma}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
such that

\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}r(\theta)\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)<\infty

(4.2)

and

\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}(1+\theta)^{-\alpha}r(\theta)|M_{b}(\theta)|^{2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)<\infty,\ \ \int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}(1+\theta)^{1-\alpha}r(\theta)|M_{\sigma}(\theta)|^{2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)<\infty

(4.3)

for some
\alpha\in[0,1)
, where
r(\theta):=1\wedge(\theta^{-1/2})
.

In the following, when we impose
Assumption

2
on the kernels
K_{b}
and
K_{\sigma}
, we fix
\mu
,
M_{b}
,
M_{\sigma}
and
\alpha
appearing therein and use the notation
r(\theta):=1\wedge(\theta^{-1/2})
. The class of the kernels satisfying
Assumption

2
is characterized by the following lemma.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
\alpha\in[0,1)
. The kernels
K_{b},K_{\sigma}:(0,T)\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
satisfy
Assumption

2
if and only if they are in form of

K_{b}(t)=\big(K^{i,j,+}_{b}(t)-K^{i,j,-}_{b}(t)\big)^{n}_{i,j=1}\ \ \text{and}\ \ K_{\sigma}(t)=\big(K^{i,j,+}_{\sigma}(t)-K^{i,j,-}_{\sigma}(t)\big)^{n}_{i,j=1},\ \ t\in(0,T),

(4.4)

for some completely monotone functions
1
1

1

A (scalar) function
k:(0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
is said to be completely monotone if it is infinitely differentiable and such that
(-1)^{m}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}t^{m}}k(t)\geq 0
for any
t\in(0,\infty)
and any nonnegative integer
m
.

K^{i,j,\pm}_{b},K^{i,j,\pm}_{\sigma}:(0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that

\begin{split}&\int^{T}_{0}t^{-\frac{1}{2}}K^{i,j,\pm}_{b}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty\ \ \text{and}\ \ \lim_{t\downarrow 0}K^{i,j,\pm}_{\sigma}(t)<\infty\ \ \text{for any $i,j\in\{1,\dots,n\}$ if $\alpha=0$, and}\\
&\int^{T}_{0}t^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2}}K^{i,j,\pm}_{b}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty\ \ \text{and}\ \ \int^{T}_{0}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}K^{i,j,\pm}_{\sigma}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty\ \ \text{for any $i,j\in\{1,\dots,n\}$ if $\alpha\in(0,1)$.}\end{split}

(4.5)

Furthermore, under
Assumption

2
,
K_{b}\in L^{\frac{2}{1+\alpha}}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
and
K_{\sigma}\in L^{\frac{2}{\alpha}}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
.

Remark 4.2
.

Notice that, by Hölder’s inequality, (
4.5
) holds if
K^{i,j,\pm}_{b}\in\bigcup_{\delta>0}L^{\frac{2}{1+\alpha}+\delta}(0,T;\mathbb{R})
and
K^{i,j,\pm}_{\sigma}\in\bigcup_{\delta>0}L^{\frac{2}{\alpha}+\delta}(0,T;\mathbb{R})
for any
i,j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
.

Proof of
Lemma

4.1
.

Suppose that
K_{b}
and
K_{\sigma}
satisfy
Assumption

2
. For each
i,j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, define

K^{i,j,\pm}_{b}(t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}e^{-\theta t}\,\mu^{i,j,\pm}_{b}(\mathrm{d}\theta),\ \ K^{i,j,\pm}_{\sigma}(t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}e^{-\theta t}\,\mu^{i,j,\pm}_{\sigma}(\mathrm{d}\theta),\ \ t\in(0,\infty),

(4.6)

where, for
\varphi\in\{b,\sigma\}
,
\mu^{i,j,+}_{\varphi}
and
\mu^{i,j,-}_{\varphi}
are Borel measures on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
defined by
